Player Ratings West Ham v Cardiff: Bowen Inspires Incredible Comeback

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

West Ham produced an extraordinary second-half comeback to rescue a 3-3 draw against Cardiff City, having looked completely shell-shocked as they trudged off at half-time three goals behind. Scott Parker’s first game in charge certainly wasn’t short of incident, with the Hammers seemingly heading for another humiliating defeat.

Joel Moylan’s first-half hat-trick had left West Ham in all sorts of trouble, but three substitutions at the interval helped transform the performance. Goals from Divine Mukasa, Arne Engels and Max Kilman completed a remarkable recovery, with the Hammers almost snatching a winner during a frantic finish.

Jarrod Bowen was the inspiration behind the comeback, although the difference between West Ham’s first and second-half performances was astonishing. Having looked completely devoid of confidence during the opening 45 minutes, the players suddenly discovered some urgency, purpose and attacking quality.

It was certainly a dramatic response to the dreadful performance against West Bromwich Albion, although Parker will have seen plenty of problems that still need addressing.

Made a couple of decent saves and produced one absolutely brilliant long throw that almost released Castellanos. Had little chance with Cardiff’s opening goal, but his decision to charge out of his penalty area, miss the ball completely and collide with Kilman was unnecessarily rash. Another unconvincing performance.

Steady and assured defensively in the first half, but his understanding with Bowen was almost nonexistent. The pair looked like they’d never met each other before. Completely different after the break, when Veltman played with real intensity, provided attacking width and combined excellently with the captain. One of West Ham’s most improved performers.

Some excellent defending, including a particularly impressive one-on-one challenge late in the game. His distribution was also a major positive, regularly pinging accurate balls towards the left flank and producing a lovely through ball to create Kanté’s one-on-one opportunity. One of West Ham’s better players across the 90 minutes.

Made an excellent early tackle but was subsequently booked after Solomon’s dreadful loss of possession exposed the defence. May have got a slight deflection on Cardiff’s opening goal, although there was little he could realistically have done. More than made amends in the 81st minute, showing West Ham’s strikers how to finish with a superb half-volley to equalise.

Actually started very well, winning numerous tackles and recovering possession repeatedly. Unfortunately, his lack of pace was badly exposed by a simple ball over the top, leading to a clumsy and avoidable penalty. He’s a centre-back being asked to play left-back, and it showed. Replaced at half-time.

Made his fair share of tackles and demonstrated some of the value of having a specialist defensive midfielder. Produced a crucial last-ditch challenge to prevent Cardiff from potentially scoring again. However, West Ham still left far too much space in central areas and he was withdrawn at half-time.

Worked hard in a box-to-box role but offered surprisingly little of note for much of the match. Played a decent pass in the build-up to one of Kanté’s attacking runs before making a beautifully timed run beyond Cardiff’s defence to score his first West Ham goal. Took the finish very well, and it was a significant contribution.

West Ham’s most technically accomplished player in the first half, looking comfortable in possession and enjoying the freedom of playing as a number ten. Picked out some lovely passes, including an excellent through ball that Castellanos couldn’t capitalise upon. Took his goal brilliantly, controlling Bowen’s chipped pass with his right foot before finishing delicately with his left. A very encouraging performance.

His performance was a microcosm of West Ham’s entire evening. Anonymous and nervous in the first half, but absolutely commanding in the second. His clever chipped pass created Mukasa’s goal, his long ball started the move for Engels’ strike, and his inswinging delivery provided the equaliser for Kilman.

By the closing stages he was practically running the game, producing a wonderful piece of jiggery-pokery to create a late chance for Mayers before delivering another dangerous inswinging ball that Souček headed on target. Outstanding after the break.

Showed some early purpose with a good run down the left, but his decision-making was dreadful. Repeatedly hesitated when presented with opportunities to shoot or pass, and one particularly poor three-yard ball straight to an opponent led to Kilman’s booking. His final deliveries were ineffective and he struggled to make the most of promising positions. Replaced after 67 minutes, and not before time.

Pretty much anonymous throughout the first half. Repeatedly outpaced when through balls offered him opportunities to run beyond Cardiff’s defence, including one occasion when he was beaten for speed by centre-back Will Fish. Managed a tame shot just before the interval but offered very little. Admittedly, West Ham’s disjointed attacking play hardly helped him. Replaced at half-time.

At least attempted to drop deep and get involved, although his first couple of contributions involved giving the ball away. Headed a decent Veltman cross straight at Trott and failed to connect properly with another Bowen delivery. Improved considerably, producing a good shot from distance before supplying an excellent flick-on for Engels’ goal.

Brought tremendous energy and directness after coming on at half-time. Produced an excellent driving run almost half the length of the pitch, followed by some lovely footwork to twist and turn through Cardiff’s defence. Gave the ball away cheaply on occasion but also forced Trott into a very good save after a wonderful first touch created a one-on-one opportunity. Probably did enough to force his way into Parker’s thoughts for the next game.

Introduced at half-time to replace Morato and provide some much-needed pace at full-back. Won an early tackle and looked more comfortable dealing with the demands of the position. Almost scored a dramatic late winner after making an excellent run to meet Bowen’s chipped delivery at the far post, attempting a left-footed instep volley that went tantalisingly close.

Made more of an attacking impression in his first 30 seconds than Solomon had managed for much of the evening. Immediately dropped his shoulder, cut inside and tried his luck with a shot that went wide. In the 89th minute, produced a stinging effort that Trott could only parry back into his penalty area. Confident, positive and willing to take responsibility.

Introduced in the 88th minute for Veltman. Almost scored a dramatic stoppage-time winner when he met another dangerous inswinging Bowen delivery with a well-directed header, forcing Nathan Trott into an excellent save. Not on the pitch long enough to warrant a numerical rating.

Kuyt revels in Fener’s special night as Iraola bemoans set-piece sloppoiness

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Dirk Kuyt celebrated a special night for Fenerbahce, as he believes the Turkish giants demonstrated what a "wonderful club" they are in their win over his old team Liverpool.

Kuyt spent six years at Liverpool, reaching the Champions League final with them in 2007.

Yet he lined up against the Reds on Wednesday, as he is in interim charge of Fener.

And the Dutchman led Fener to their first Champions League victory in 18 years as Vedat Muriqi's 79th-minute header clinched a 3-2 win.

Liverpool twice went ahead in Istanbul, through Trey Nyoni's first senior strike and Alexander Isak's maiden Champions League goal for the Reds.

Yet Fener twice fought back, with Mason Greenwood and Archie Brown on target before Muriqi's first Champions League goal settled the contest.

"It was a very special night for me and everyone who loves Fenerbahce," said Kuyt.

"We had some difficult times but I have to give a very big compliment to the players of Fenerbahce and also the staff members who keep pushing the players.

"We told each other to stick together, be united and keep believing in ourselves. That's something I learned from another club that means a lot to me and that is Liverpool, who we played tonight.

"I'm very happy with all the players, all the people involved, that we made a miracle tonight because not many people expected us to win. But we said before the game that we have a chance if we show passion, energy; if we stick to our game plan then the crowd will come behind us.

"We showed Europe what a wonderful club and what wonderful supporters that Fenerbahce has."

Fenerbahçe 3-2 Liverpool A fifth consecutive away defeat in the UCL for Liverpool against a Turkish club. Fenerbahçe were 2-1 down with just 17 minutes to go, but goals from Archie Brown & Vedat Muriqi turn the game around in their favour.

— Opta Analyst (@OptaAnalyst) November 4, 2026

Isak gave Liverpool a 2-1 lead in the 69th minute, meaning it is the latest they have ever gone ahead in a major European game and then lost.

Liverpool conceded three goals from set pieces in a Champions League game for just the second time, along with their 3-3 draw with Sevilla in November 2017, though this was the first time ever they have let in three non-penalty set-piece goals in one game.

"The main reason that I can find for why we lost this game is we conceded from three set pieces. It's something that we were doing very well in," he said.

"We knew it was one of the strengths of Fenerbahce. We concede from a free-kick, a corner and even from the long throw at the end.

"They're goals that have hurt us because we've gone twice in front after having good moments, controlling the game. We've found the goals but we have conceded in situations we should definitely defend better.

"It's frustrating because it's a difficult place to come here and play. In most parts of the game we were thinking we could win this one but obviously it hasn't happened.

"Especially the first goal and the third goal – the second one is a rebound – but the first one, I don't think we set the wall properly and the last one, Muriqi wins the ball against three defenders; it's definitely something we need to defend better."

Spalletti ‘happy to give a bit more responsibility’ to Juve youngster Alajbegovic

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Luciano Spalletti believes youngster Kerim Alajbegovic has earned "a bit more responsibility" ahead of Juventus' Europa League trip to AZ.

Alajbegovic scored his first Serie A goal for the Bianconeri in their 2-0 win over Napoli on Sunday.

At 19 years and 41 days old, he became the youngest non-Italian goalscorer in the history of the Serie A fixture between Juve and Napoli.

Alajbegovic is also the youngest forward to have both scored at least one goal and provided at least one assist in Europe's top-five leagues in the 2026-27 season.

In total, five forwards born in 2007 or later have both scored and assisted in these leagues this term: alongside the Bosnian, they are Lamine Yamal, Charalampos Kostoulas, Jay Robinson, and Paul Mendy.

Spalletti has been surprised by Alajbegovic's early impact, but has tempered expectations around the teenager.

"Alajbegovic took on the responsibility; a young player like him usually slides back in difficult moments, but instead he gave an important contribution to turn those difficulties around," said Spalletti.

"Lost his spark..." - Vinicius Jr handed brutal verdict by Spanish media despite Real Madrid win

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

By Lewis Blain | 04 Nov 2026 21:32

Real Madrid maintained their perfect start in the 2026-27 Champions League league phase with a 3-2 victory over AEK Athens on Wednesday evening, but Vinicius Junior failed to take advantage of an opportunity to impress Jose Mourinho.

The Brazilian was handed a chance to strengthen his claims for a regular starting position ahead of his return from domestic suspension, but delivered another underwhelming performance on a night where Yan Diomande continued to enhance his reputation.

Vinicius's display attracted widespread criticism in Spain, with one publication awarding him just 2/10, while several prominent journalists questioned whether he deserves to remain in Madrid's strongest starting XI.

ElDesmarque were particularly scathing, awarding Vinicius just 2/10 and criticising his lack of attacking influence and defensive contribution. They highlighted his failure to track back, the space he left behind him and the fact that he completed none of his attempted dribbles.

AS were similarly unimpressed, describing his first half as ineffective and his second-half efforts as "un quiero y no puedo" - 'trying but unable to deliver'.

Former Real Madrid player and coach Jorge Valdano felt that "La palabra aceptable no es para un jugador como Vinicius" - 'the word acceptable does not apply to a player like Vinicius' - adding that he had produced just one good attacking move.

AS deputy editor Tomas Roncero was even more direct, declaring "Vini tenía una opción para lucirse, pero nada" - Vini had an opportunity to shine, but 'delivered nothing'. He subsequently questioned whether the Brazilian deserved to start, claiming that he had "perdido la luz" - 'lost his spark'.

Cadena SER's Dani Garrido agreed that Vinicius had wasted his opportunity, while Real Madrid correspondent Javier Herraez offered a pointed comparison with his rival for a starting position: "Diomande es un ejemplo, aprende de él Vini" - 'Diomande is an example, learn from him, Vini.'

The growing statistical gap between Vinicius and Diomande makes the criticism particularly difficult to dismiss.

According to figures highlighted by El Pais, Vinicius has attempted 37 dribbles this season, successfully completing just 15 at a rate of 40%. Diomande, meanwhile, has attempted 56 and completed 36, boasting a considerably better success rate of 64%.

Even more concerning is that Vinicius has gone two consecutive Champions League matches without attempting a single dribble, an extraordinary statistic for someone whose explosive running and ability to beat defenders have traditionally been his biggest strengths.

Diomande is therefore offering Mourinho an increasingly convincing alternative, with AS suggesting that the summer signing is already several lengths ahead in the battle for a starting position.

One disappointing performance would not normally threaten the status of a player with Vinicius's achievements, but the mounting criticism suggests that his problems are becoming a recurring issue rather than an isolated loss of form.

The Brazilian still possesses the quality to turn things around, but he needs to rediscover his confidence, directness and defensive commitment sooner rather than later.

With Diomande making a compelling case to remain in the team, Vinicius can no longer rely solely on his reputation to guarantee a starting position at the Bernabeu.

Harriet Phillips Says Raising Her Daughter Solo Was “Painful.” But Now She Can Ask Kate Middleton for Advice

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Harriet Phillips, the wife of Peter Phillips, son of Princess Anne, sat down with Hello! for an exclusive interview five months after her royal wedding, which took place at All Saints’ Church in Kemble, Gloucestershire, the village where she grew up. The ceremony was attended by King Charles III and Queen Camilla, as well as many other members of the royal family.

For Harriet, who until then led a life far removed from high society, her official entry into one of the world’s most famous royal families inevitably brought about some big changes.

Harriet and Peter Phillips at Royal Ascot

She and her daughter, Georgina, now live at Gatcombe, Princess Anne’s estate. Peter’s sister, Zara Tindall, also lives there with her husband, Mike, and their three children. After 24 years with the British National Health Service (NHS), where she specialized in child mental health and caring for the most vulnerable children, Harriet left the NHS to embark on a new professional venture at a private foundation, continuing to focus on early-childhood development.

She has also taken on her first official role as patron of Home-Start Cotswolds, an organization that supports families with young children. “Becoming a parent can be one of the most wonderful experiences in life, but it can also feel overwhelming and isolating,” Phillips told Hello! “We often assume that parenting comes naturally, but the reality is that everyone needs support at different points. Home-Start offers vital, practical, and nonjudgmental support. Just having someone to listen can make an enormous difference.”

For Harriet, the decision to leave the health service does not represent a break with the past: “It’s been an enormous privilege to spend 24 years working in the NHS, and I’ve loved my career. It has taught me so much, professionally and personally. Over time, I’ve become increasingly passionate about the importance of promoting healthy brain development. If we can support parents during this critical early period, we have an opportunity to make a lasting difference for children.”

The Phillips couple at the 2026 DMMI Royal Charity Polo Cup

Harriet then looked back on her love story with Peter, which began in an entirely unexpected way. The two first met properly in early 2022, when their three daughters were on the same hockey team. At first there was just some casual small talk between them, then walks with their dogs, followed by dinners at pubs away from prying eyes and, finally, evenings spent cooking together. A low-key courtship, away from the spotlight, blossomed into a love story.

When she met Peter, there wasn’t a “thunderbolt moment”; Harriet said she simply felt comfortable with him, enjoying an unspoken sense of calm. “I just felt incredibly at ease with him and experienced a lovely, peaceful, safe feeling when talking to him—like being with a really good friend,” she told Hello!

He was the one who made the first move: “Peter was the brave one in recognizing that there was something between us and acting on it. This time, I thought, If I’m lucky enough to meet someone again, I want things to move slowly and ensure that everything is entirely right.” Then she added, unable to hide her happiness: “He is very romantic, loving, kind, loyal, amazing, and my best friend.” Their early dates were simple and low-key. “He’s a great cook and I love cooking. He’s great at barbecues. It was wholesome and suited to who we both are as people,” she said.

On July 25, 2025, during a weekend in the south of England, Peter proposed on a beach. “We were on a weekend getaway in the south of England on a hot day during the heat wave. He asked me to marry him on the beach; I was completely overcome,” she said. “He had the ring made at Pragnell beforehand—it’s absolutely beautiful.” Neither of their families had been told about the surprise: “It was the best-kept secret ever.”

During the interview, Harriet also reflected on her childhood in Gloucestershire and fondly remembered her parents and their long marriage. “I was brought up in a village in the middle of fields. My parents celebrated an incredible 51 years of marriage, and sadly, my father died just before Peter and I got together.” It was an experience that left a deep mark on her life, and today, as she said in reflecting on her own love story, Harriet also sees it in the context of meeting the Princess Royal’s son: “I often think maybe that’s why Peter came along.”

The loss of her father, and the difficulties she faced in the years that followed, helped shape her outlook on life. Harriet explained: “There is a beauty that comes out of difficult situations. A beautiful jewel is formed under pressure. Difficulties in life enable you to relate to people and build empathy.”

Harriet and Peter with Princess Royal Anne

Harriet then talked about her first meeting with Princess Anne, her eventual mother-in-law, which was much more informal than she had imagined it would be. They met in the garden at Gatcombe, sitting on benches and drinking cups of tea. “There was always going to be initial nerves about meeting anyone’s family members, but I am quite a calm person,” she said. “It was really relaxed…It couldn’t have been nicer.”

Harriet has come to appreciate, above all, the princess’s dedication to her work and local charities: “She sets an amazing example with her work ethic. What I’ve seen is her tireless attention to local charities and her genuine interests. It’s important to do things that match who you are, where you live, and what your passions are.”

And their relationship already seems very strong. “She’s brilliant, really good fun, and straight-talking,” Harriet said. “She’s incredibly generous—we had our wedding reception at Gatcombe, and it was amazing and special for Peter and I to have our wedding where he grew up.”

Charles and Camilla also welcomed her warmly right from the start, as seen in the various photos taken at public events. Her first meeting with the king and the queen consort took place at Royal Ascot: “It was so lovely and relaxed and there was lots of laughter. You feel as though you’re meeting family, but at the same time, it feels otherworldly. Both the king and the queen have been so welcoming to me and Georgina. I can’t thank them both enough.”

The couple with the king and queen at Royal Ascot

After their wedding, Harriet and Peter attended several public events, including Wimbledon. In September, they were together at Somerset House in London for the opening of the exhibition “Reviving Craft: Reflection of the Mind,” dedicated to traditional Chinese craftsmanship and featuring over 70 artists.

Harriet also spoke with Hello! about Kate Middleton, including the admiration she feels for her and the bond they’ve formed. “I can look to Catherine for advice and guidance,” she said. She recalled a special moment during a vacation on the Isles of Scilly: The children (Georgina, George, Charlotte, and Louis) found themselves playing in the water together, in a spontaneous and informal atmosphere. “I’ve had an incredible experience being welcomed in. We happened to be on holiday in the same place. It was great because the children were all jumping into the water at the same time,” she shared. “It was very casual, but there is a clear level of respect there.” And speaking of the Princess of Wales, she added: “She’s very openhearted, and I would always feel comfortable asking her if I needed advice about something.”

Her first stay at Balmoral with her daughter was also a special experience. Scotland is one of Harriet’s favorite destinations, and vacations with the royal family have remained surprisingly simple: “It’s the loveliest family holiday with an extraordinary layer…. We go walking and running, play football with the children, cycle in the hills, and go wild swimming, which is fantastic.”

Then, inevitably, her memories turned to the wedding, an event marked by emotional moments. Harriet got ready with her mother and the three girls in the family: Georgina, Savannah, and Isla. Then her brother, Nicholas, arrived to drive her to the church. “It was quite wet and it had rained that morning, but the girls kept my train completely white and immaculate,” said said. “They did a really good job.”

In the church, decorated with floral arrangements that seemed to bring nature indoors, Harriet carried a bouquet with myrtle and lily of the valley: “All my family members have had myrtle in their wedding flowers, so I continued that tradition.”

Before going in, the couple prayed together with the vicar. “Then [we] walked into the fragrance of the flowers in the church. It was incredible,” she said. “It’s such a beautiful church, and I grew up going to it. It’s tiny—we were all squished in!” The presence of the king, the queen, and other members of the royal family didn’t distract her: “I was so focused on being in the moment. Of course, you’re aware that people are around you, but I really wanted to enjoy it and be present. I was so happy. That’s very natural for me. I was peaceful. I prayed. Maybe the peace of God came over me.”

The reception took place in the garden at Gatcombe. The sun came out at one point; there was live music, Champagne, and a casual menu featuring lamb raised on the estate. Instead of the traditional wedding cake, there was a huge tiramisu. “There was lots of laughter and it was an amazing family day. It finished at midnight, but everyone wanted it to carry on,” she said.

Before meeting Peter, Harriet was a single mother raising her preteen daughter, Georgina. It wasn’t always an easy journey, which made her particularly sensitive to the challenges that parents face and the importance of having a support network.

“Although at times it was a painful experience, I did have the support of people who loved us. Any time in life when you’re under pressure or facing adversity, something good always comes from it,” she said. “Lean in; don’t be scared.” Her Christian faith played an important role—“It’s definitely the foundation of how I live; it’s like a golden thread that has woven through my life. I was brought up in a Christian family, but this is a little bit different because it’s like making a decision for yourself: It is a personal relationship and a conscious choice, rather than just being religious.”

Harriet is very proud of her daughter and the relationship they’ve built. “Becoming a mother gives you an inner strength to protect your loved one and face whatever comes,” she said. “Everyone has challenges in life that are dressed up differently. Maybe this has been my challenge—the experience of bringing up a child on my own. I’ve been able to see the good things that have come out of it.”

Harriet and Peter with their three daughters on their wedding day

Today her family has grown, and Harriet looks to the future with enthusiasm, but without rushing: “Peter and I are so fortunate to have such wonderful girls between us. I’m excited about what’s to come with our family and always mindful that going gently is so important.”

Her new life with Peter doesn’t mean setting aside her own identity. “I will continue as I am. I’m a professional and will continue working in the area I’m passionate about,” she said. “I’ve got a wealth of knowledge that I don’t want to waste, so I will continue to use that.” When asked what she’d like to accomplish in the next 12 months, she gave an answer that once again came back to children and families: raising greater awareness of the work of Home-Start Cotswolds. As she explained, “We don’t just support babies by caring for them directly—we support babies by supporting the people who care for them.”

It’s a philosophy that also seems to capture her new sense of balance in her life. She has joined the royal family without ceasing to be Harriet, the woman who for 24 years worked alongside children and their parents, and who today has found in Peter a life partner and, above all, a best friend.

Her mission at Home-Start brings her even closer to the Princess of Wales, who has also been committed for years to supporting the well-being of children in their early years, a crucial period that shapes their personalities and determines the adults they will become. Who knows if the two women, now close friends, might one day form a professional partnership as well.

Harriet with Peter, Zara, and Mike Tindall.

Originally published by Vanity Fair Italia.

Matthew Stafford's wife calls out NFL over apparent scheduling disadvantage given to Rams

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

For the first time ever, the NFL will play a game the night before Thanksgiving, with the Los Angeles Rams hosting the Green Bay Packers in a rare Wednesday matchup on Nov. 25 at 8 p.m. ET on Netflix.

To make the game work on the schedule, both teams were essentially forced to sacrifice their bye week, and Kelly Stafford definitely isn't happy about that fact. The wife of Matthew Stafford took to social media this week to call out the NFL for putting the Rams at a scheduling disadvantage compared to the league's other teams.

"Just curious to hear the thought process behind making the Rams the only team in the NFL without a true bye week," Kelly wrote on Instagram. "Because damn, after 18 years around this league, even I need a week off during the season...and I have never played a single snap."

Normally, a team goes 14 days between games during a bye week, but the Rams will only be getting 10 days off. Here's what their schedule looks like in the lead-up to their Thanksgiving Eve showdown with the Packers:

Week 10 (Nov. 15): at Cardinals (Sunday)Week 11 (Nov. 22): BYEWeek 12 (Nov. 25): Packers (Wednesday)Week 13 (Dec. 3): Chiefs (Thursday)

The Packers will be in a similar situation, but they get a slightly more favorable schedule since they don't have to play on Thursday night in Week 13. Instead, the Packers will play on Sunday, Dec. 6, so Green Bay will play only one game over a 19-day period. They still won't have a full bye, but it's better than what the Rams have.

Here's what Green Bay's schedule looks like in that same window:

Week 10 (Nov. 15): Vikings (Sunday)Week 11 (Nov. 22): BYEWeek 12 (Nov. 25): at Rams (Wednesday)Week 13 (Dec. 6): at Saints (Sunday)

NFL teams look forward to the bye each year so they can rest and heal, but Rams and Packers players won't be getting the full week off in 2026.

"I'm tired just watching from the stands," Kelly wrote. "Can't imagine how the guys feel."

Kelly then ended things by asking the NFL to explain why the Rams got hosed over.

"Anyway, feel free to leave that explanation right here," Stafford said.

The Rams were given one of the wildest schedules in the NFL this year. Not only are they playing a Wednesday game, but they also have a Friday game against the Seahawks on Christmas Day. If the Rams end up playing a Saturday game, which could happen if they get flexed in Week 17 or Week 18, they'd become just the fourth team in 99 years to play a game on six different days of the week during a single season.

The Rams have been the NFL's guinea pigs this year. They also played in the inaugural Australia game back in Week 1. Thanks to that trip, the Rams are scheduled to travel more than 34,000 miles this season, which is the second-most in the NFL.

Although Kelly Stafford isn't happy about the Rams losing their bye week, the Rams will be home for Thanksgiving. That means they'll get to spend the holiday weekend with their families, so the team's odd schedule isn't a total loss.

Barcelona v Aston Villa: Champions League – live

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

⚽️ Champions League updates from the 8pm GMT kick-off ⚽️ Manchester United v Roma – follow it live ⚽️ Live scores | League phase standings | And email Luke

40 min: “Aside from giving it away on the edge of the box, Villa have done great,” says Gerrard on commentary. Which is a fair point. They’ve looked good, they’ve tried to play and created chances. But the reckless playing it out from the back has to stop.

38 min: Lamine Yamal is good at football. Granted, everyone out there on the pitch is, but he really is a class apart. The problem for Villa is he can create chances for himself and others seemingly effortlessly, and no matter how much pressure Villa apply at the other end, Barcelona have a world-class outlet to take advantage of at their leisure. Right on cue, Lamine Yamal produces a ridiculous nutmeg pass, angled from the right wing, to create yet another decent chance.

36 min: Jackson finds yet more space down the Villa right. He’s one-on-one with Fariñas. Manzambi is screaming for it at the far post … Jackson’s eventual shot is deflected out for a corner. The set-piece drops dangerously in the six-yard box but there is no Villa foot or head there to meet it.

34 min: The vast majority of Barcelona’s attack has gone down the right, mostly through Lamine Yamal. But now it’s Raphinha, who pounces on a loose ball near halfway, and streaks into the Villa area. He nearly fashions an opportunity to shoot but Villa manage to deal with it. Just.

31 min: Manzambi goes close for Villa! Barcelona are being generous at the back, in terms of allowing these Villa forwards space to run at them. Manzambi cuts in and out, creating a chance for himself, and hits a shot straight at García! Good from Villa, good from the Switzerland international.

30 min: López battles hard to win the ball back high for Barcelona, and is then clattered by Buendía. The referee books the Argentinian at the earliest opportunity.

On the touchline, Emery looks deeply concerned, and you can understand why.

28 min: Lamine Yamal bends a ludicrous pass inside, behind the Villa back line, looking for Raphinha. Is he offside? It’s academic, because his touch is heavy and he cannot control the ball. Lovely delivery by Lamine Yamal all the same.

26 min: Oh my. Oh dear! What are Villa doing? What sort of thing is happening here? Suzuki tries to play it out from the back again and it’s obvious, before he even does it, what the upshot is going to be. Sure enough the ball is lost on the edge, and Gordon hammers a shot from an angle, that is saved in desperate fashion by the Villa goalie.

24 min: Lamine Yamal is making short work of Maatsen down the Barcelona right, thus far, it is true.

My team talk for Villa (like it always in the glory days at five-a-side) would have been: “Just keep it tight at the back early on, lads.” To be fair, that would have been very useful advice.

23 min: “This new iteration of the Villa team has come together faster than I could possibly have imagined,” emails Benjamin. “But our defence could have done with strengthening even before the summer’s team evisceration, and where Cash, Lindelof and Pau are all very good - on their day - this is not a wholly confident Villa fan assuming my stool in the Frog & Fiddle.

“While Gordon has stepped up magnificently post-move, and is legitimately a part of an intimidating Barça attack, Cash has the chops to match him… Maatsen vs Yamal, though … stuff of nightmares!

“This will be a fascinating test of this nascent team. Our season doesn’t hinge on this, but a win would probably outstrip the achievements of the prior Champion’s League campaign.”

20 min: Villa counter down the right, and quickly. Manzambi plays Jackson in, and he’s in acres of space due to Barcelona’s high line: the forward suddenly has just the goalie to beat! But García comes way, way out of his area and puts in a vital tackle.

19 min: Another chance for Villa! Manzambi meets a square ball from Jackson, from the Villa right, but he scuffs it horribly! McGinn did well to step over the ball and leave it for Manzambi there.

16 min: A dangerous run by Gordon down the hosts’ left. He squares the ball low but neither Raphinha nor Lamine Yamal can meet the cross with an effort on goal.

They replay the goal on telly: Lamine Yamal was exceptionally alert to the possibility of Joan García playing the horrible pass that he did after the pass out from Suzuki. It was so naive from Villa. And Emery will be disgusted to have conceded such a soft goal. Still, there is still hope …

14 min: McGinn pumps a ball down into the left channel looking for the run of Jackson. Joan García, the goalie, plays sweeper and deals with it easily. Still, Villa don’t look intimidated, even if they’ve handed Barcelona a one-goal lead on a silver platter.

13 min: Lamine Yamal sprints to the byline again with the ball under his command. But then, the ball sneaks out for a goal kick before he squares it.

11 min: Maatsen goes in the book after Lamine Yamal mugs him off, touching the ball past the Villa defender, and going to ground. Now a nervy disciplinary tightrope to walk for the full-back for a mere 80min or so …

9 min: Chances for Villa to hit back immediately! Cash hammers a volley just wide! Another corner – and João Gomes’s point-blank range effort is blocked by Cancelo! That was a brave block by the Barça defender.

Villa look to play it out from the back. Suzuki passes to João Gomes, on the edge of the area, who plays a loose first-time pass that is pounced on by Lamine Yamal. The Spain international doesn’t need a second invitation and tucks a simple finish into the bottom corner. Defensive suicide from Villa.

7 min: Raphinha’s set-piece is a low, skiddy one and flashes past the far post. Four or five Barça players may have been offside …

5 min: Lamine Yamal gets on the ball near the Villa penalty area and is barged into by Buendía. It’s a free-kick in a very handy spot for the home team … the Prince of Wales is pictured up in the stands, keeping a watchful eye on the action. Raphinha will hit the free-kick.

5 min: López escapes down the Barcelona right and squares for Gordon, whose shot is blocked. Another little warning for Villa …

3 min: McGinn has his pocket picked in a defensive area, but he gets away with it. He wasn’t alert enough there and Villa won’t be able to afford many lapses like that … Mind you, there shape and composure looks good overall, based on the very early exchanges.

2 min: The 20-year-old Brian Fariñas made his debut for Barcelona at the weekend and keeps his place tonight. I may have said it was three changes for Barça, by the way: it is in fact two – Gerard and Gordon the players coming in.

The Champions League anthem will not be played tonight. There will be an opportunity to remember Jesús de Miguel, the Tenerife player who died this week after an on-field collision, as well as the victims of last week’s train crash in Spain.

Emery’s managerial career record against Barcelona is played 30, won three, lost 21.

It’s hammering down with rain in Barcelona. Well, it is November. It’ll be a nice greasy surface for Villa’s fluid attacking game.

The players and officials are beginning to assemble in the tunnel.

We are, quite literally, less than 10 minutes away from kick-off at the Camp Nou.

There is still time for all you Villa fans to send me your hopes, dreams and fears for this box-office clash in Catalonia.

“I take 10, 20 minutes every day to sit, visualise, be calm and meditate,” Anthony Gordon tells Jules Breach, in a pre-recorded interview, on TNT Sports.

“And I am truly grateful for my life, for what I’ve created.”

“It seems quite difficult for people to understand that kind of ambition, if you don’t have it yourself … but I think if you speak to any of my family, friends, people who’ve been close to me, I’ve said this from very, very early.

“Ultimately, now, like most humans – you get to where you want to get to, and then you just want more. I need goals, I need trophies, I need everything else that comes with it.”

Matty Cash v Anthony Gordon, then, on the Barcelona left?

And Lamine Yamal v Ian Maatsen on the other flank.

It’s going to be an incredible defensive shift from Villa if they fly back to England with some sort of positive result. You better believe it!

Jules Breach and Gerrard were just having a chat for TNT Sports as the Barcelona players went out to warm up. Gerrard has never seen Lamine Yamal play in the flesh before.

Anthony Gordon spotted Gerrard and came and said hello. Lamine Yamal then immediately followed suit. Gerrard gave him a slightly awkward hug, and looked genuinely star-struck that the Spain international had come over to say hi. That’s a measure of the young man’s growing reputation right there.

“Good evening,” says Emery, speaking to TNT Sports. “Yes, we are excited …

“Today is the highest level in European football. And we are ready … but of course, we know it is going to be a very tough match … but we need to understand how we can compete. How we can exploit our capacity, our skills … and being organised.

“Barcelona are very, very competitive – favourites in this competition – they have now six points, and playing at home, after defeat against Paris Saint-Germain, they must win – it’s a good test to see how we can compete when the level is high, and when the opponent needs, need to compete against us.”

If you were unfortunate enough to miss Barney Ronay’s excellent preview … here it is again:

“European football has done a pretty good job in the past two seasons of turning its autumn fixture blizzard into a hopeless and baggy blur. What serious sport actually has a 36-team mega table? And a deeply predictable 36-team mega-table at that, one where you pretty much know who’s going to be up there at the end of it. If the intention was to create a series of exhibition-style TV-product events, thereby heading off the Super League’s desire to do exactly the same, then mission semi-accomplished.

“There are still exceptions in the period of drift, one-off events that speak to the colour and cultural weight of this competition. Tuesday night is one of them, for Villa’s fans a genuine staging point in the progress of this ever-evolving team, with a chance, dependent on results elsewhere, to actually top the Champions League mega-table at this early stage.”

Of the four sides who have three wins out of three, it’s PSG and Villa in action today. Liverpool and Real Madrid both play tomorrow.

Atlético Madrid v Bayern Munich Barcelona v Aston Villa Bodø/Glimt v Lille Feyenoord v Inter LASK v Slovan Bratislava Manchester United v Roma Villareal v Paris Saint-Germain

Steven Gerrard and Ally McCoist are on co-commentary duty for TNT Sports alongside Darren Fletcher in Barcelona. The sight and sound of Gerrard has reminded me to recap CarragherGate:

“Jamie Carragher has apologised to Aston Villa for using “clumsy and wrong” language when discussing the club, after club had threatened legal action. “I’d like to apologise to Aston Villa, the owners and their fans. My language this week was clumsy and wrong,” Carragher wrote on X on Saturday.

“I can assure Villa fans it was tongue-in-cheek but I can understand it’s not come across that way, so apologies. Good luck today UTV [Up the Villa]!”

“Aston Villa have dropped their threat of legal action against Jamie Carragher following an apology from the Sky Sports pundit.

“Villa announced on Friday that they would be taking legal action against Carragher for “unacceptable insults about the club and its fans” on the Stick to Football podcast. The former Liverpool defender had told Villa supporters to “shut the fuck up” after being criticised for suggesting Unai Emery should consider taking the Manchester United job if Michael Carrick is sacked.”

Will Unwin is blogging crisis club Manchester United v Roma here:

Emery makes two changes to the side that drew 1-1 with Fulham on Saturday: João Gomes into midfield, for Leon Goretzka, and Emi Buendía comes into the forward line, replacing Alysson.

Also two changes from Barcelona’s Hansi Flick: he brings in Gerard in central defence, for Frenkie de Jong, and Anthony Gordon for Karim Adeyemi up front.

Barcelona (4-2-3-1): Joan García; Fariñas, Cubarsi, Gerard, Cancelo; Rodri, Gonzalez; Yamal, López, Gordon; Raphinha. Subtitutes: Szczesny, Livakovic, Balde, Gavi, Gabriel Jesus, Adeyemi, de Jong, Bernal, Abdelkarim, Goren, Victor, Campos.

Aston Villa (4-2-3-1): Suzuki; Cash, Lindelof, Torres, Maatsen; Kamara, João Gomes; McGinn, Manzambi, Buendia; Jackson. Subtitutes: Bizot, Wright, Harwood-Bellis, Mings, Barkley, Garnacho, Abraham, Mbaye, Goretzka, Alysson, Hemmings.

Some Villa fans, earlier, lapping up the atmosphere in Barcelona.

“Very fortunate to be at the game tonight,” emails Dave. “The stadium and facilities around it are more like the NFL. It’s a brilliant experience.

“Villa smash and grab, sit deep and get them on the break. Ride the luck in the last 20 and get it launched.”

Brilliant – thanks for your email Dave – enjoy the occasion and good luck.

“One more match. Just one more match.” Sure, Unai. Sure. Just like all the other matches that generated a local tremor measured at 1 on the Richter scale when that seventh and final goal went in at the Camp Nou in March 2017.

“It was inevitable that Unai Emery would be asked about the Remontada game on the eve of Aston Villa’s Champions League engagement with Barcelona, his own first trip back to the Camp Nou in this competition since one of the most visceral and traumatic experiences of his managerial career.

“Emery was Paris Saint-Germain’s manager the night the Neymar-Messi-Suárez triple-godhead reversed a 4-0 first-leg quarter-final deficit in this stadium.

“Plays, novels, songs. They all have a subtext. Which I take to mean a hidden message or import of some kind. So subtext, we know. But what do you call the message or meaning that’s right there on the surface? Completely open and obvious. They never talk about that. What do you call … what’s above the subtext?”

Aston Villa are three from three in the Champions League. Unai Emery’s side could top the league phase table outright after this evening’s meeting with Barcelona. But that is only part of the story.

Emery returns to the Camp Nou – scene of one of his most traumatic nights as a manager – when in 2017 Barcelona overturned a 4-0 first-leg defeat by Paris Saint-Germain, winning 6-1 on the night, 6-5 on aggregate.

So there would be subtext aplenty without the recent furore surrounding the pundit Jamie Carragher, Villa’s threatened legal action, Carragher’s apology, Villa dropping their legal threat … not to mention the fact that Emery has been linked with the Old Trafford managerial poisoned chalice hotseat amid Michael Carrick’s ongoing struggles.

Throw in a disappointing week on the pitch for the Villains – an emphatic Carabao Cup by Bournemouth and a 1-1 draw with Fulham in the Premier League on Saturday – and it’s going to be fascinating to witness what transpires on the hallowed Camp Nou turf. Dani Olmo, Andreas Christensen, Eric García and Xavi Espart are expected to miss out for La Liga-leading Barça, while the forward Nicolas Jackson should be ready to go for Villa.

Emery knows a thing or two about competing in European knockout competitions so regardless of subtexts and subplots, this should be a cracking game of association football. Vamos!

In His Own Words, a Czech Alpinist Describes How He Survived a Nightmare on Peru’s Highest Mountain

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Honza Špička on Huascarán (Photo: Courtesy of Matěj Žák)

My name is Jan “Honza” Špička. My climbing partner, Matěj Žák, and I are two alpinists, climbers, mountaineers, skiers, and endurance athletes from Nove Straseci, a small town in the Czech Republic. Although we have known each other since childhood through our families (and the local ice hockey team), our climbing paths only crossed a few years ago. Since then, we have spent three seasons together in Peru’s Cordillera Blanca and two seasons in Argentine Patagonia. We have roped up together a few times on Czech sandstone as well. We are no professionals, though; rather than extreme lines, what we have behind us are moderately difficult routes climbed in our own style: clean and fast.

Our speciality? Long days in the mountains and a fast, lightweight approach. We take nothing on an expedition that we cannot carry on our own backs.

Huascarán is the highest mountain in Peru and the highest point in the entire tropics. Its south summit reaches 6,768m and the north summit 6,655m; the two are separated by a broad col called La Garganta (“the Throat” in Spanish) at roughly 6,000m. Huascarán lies at the heart of the Cordillera Blanca, the most heavily glaciated tropical mountain range in the world, and it gave its name to the surrounding national park, a UNESCO World Heritage Site since 1985. Because the Earth bulges slightly at the equator, the gravitational acceleration on the summit of Huascarán is among the lowest anywhere on the planet’s surface; a person there measurably weighs a little less than at sea level, by a matter of grams. The summit of Huascarán is also the second most distant point on Earth from the planet’s center, right after Chimborazo.

The mountain is named after Huáscar, the Inca ruler who fought his half-brother Atahualpa for several years over control of the Inca Empire. Atahualpa eventually defeated his brother and had him executed. The American mountaineer Annie Smith Peck, then over fifty years old, climbed the north summit in 1908. The higher south summit held out until 1932, when a German–Austrian expedition made the first ascent.

Huascarán, however, also has a darker side to its history. In 1962, a huge mass of ice broke away from the mountain, and the resulting debris avalanche swept down onto the towns of Ranrahirca. Then, on May 31, 1970, an earthquake off the coast triggered a far larger and more extensive avalanche from the north summit, which within minutes buried, among other places, the entire town of Yungay. The earthquake and avalanche combined took the lives of approximately 67,000 people. It was the deadliest single avalanche disaster by death toll in recorded history.

This tragic event unfortunately also carries a strong Czechoslovak mark. It was right here, in the valley between Huascarán’s north summit and Huandoy Sur, that a Czechoslovak expedition was camped—and the whole expedition became a victim of this mountain.

If we take into account the victims of 1970 and all the climbers who have died on its slopes, it is probably the mountain responsible for more human lives than any other in modern history.

The ascent itself, via the Normal Route, is not particularly difficult, but Huascarán’s glaciers are retreating fast, and so today some routes that were relatively straightforward climbs just a generation ago involve significant objective hazards. In the six years that I have lived in Peru, I cannot remember a year when Huascarán did not claim another victim. There have even been years when it allowed no one at all to reach its summit.

The start of our trip began on Friday, July 11, in the evening in our favourite “pollo a la brasa” restaurant. Our whole group, which we called the “Cayesh crew,” met for a shared dinner to load up on a solid dose of protein before what was to be the main climbing objective of the season for all of us: Huascarán Sur. It has to be said, though, that for none of us was Huascarán the center of the universe. We all had it in “one day, maybe, I’d like to, if it comes to it…” mode, but there was certainly no obsession with this “fat one.” I already had three unsuccessful attempts behind me, but even so I wasn’t insisting on “bagging it.” Yet somehow the appetite, the motivation, the hunger for it arrived. And essentially by coincidence it arrived not only for Matěj and me but also for our friends from Peru. In the end we formed three teams of two.

I have known Saul—formally, Freddy Saul Mendoza Lizana—since 2021; he was essentially my first climbing partner here in Peru. Since then we have been through a lot together—in the mountains, on the crags, at home, and in the pub. He was like my Peruvian brother. We were so close that my wife Alča and I were already counting on a little room for Saul for when we one day return to the Czech Republic and settle down somewhere.

Agnieszka is a Polish “machine” with several 8a/5.13b sport routes to her name. She had seen plenty in the mountains, too. Definitely no lightweight.

Alejandro “Chicho” Ugarte Jordan was a professional photographer from Lima. His main interest was unique mountain images captured with his DSLR or his drone. We had known each other since 2022 through the Skiing Peru project, which we were both part of. We often spent time together with our girlfriends in Lima outside the season, too.

Artidoro “Arti” Salas Gaitan was a pure Huaracino—someone who comes from Huaraz. He was born in the town at the foot of the Cordillera Blanca and lived there his whole life. He ran the Big Mountain hostel, well known among climbers. I personally have known him since 2021, when I spent my first season in Huaraz at his hostel.

Our expedition began on the morning of Saturday, July 11, when we loaded the van with heavy packs. Under the watchful eye of—as Matěj called him—our adoptive father Paul, our friend and the owner of the cozy Cayesh Guest House bed and breakfast, we set off for our starting point, the village of Armapampa in the Yungay area. From Huaraz, it was about a two-hour drive. Besides the six of us, Alenka came along, too, having arrived from Lima that same morning, as did Míša, a Czech woman living in Nepal who had just popped over to Peru to see friends. The girls, however, were only going on a day trip to the hut and back down the next day.

On the edge of the beautiful village of Armapampa, we unloaded our gear from the van, ate the last gluten-free cinnamon rolls that Alenka baked for the trip, and shouldered our heavy packs. Matěj took a group photo and we set off towards Refugio Huascarán, our base camp. We reached the refugio at 4,650m spread out over quite a wide time range (Matěj first, at 2 hours 45 minutes, and Saul and Aga last at a little over 6 hours). Who knows where and why they lingered; apparently they were finishing off their avocados. Either way, the trail was easy and there was no rush, so everyone went at their own pace.

There was no one at the hut apart from us, so we enjoyed it immensely. The evening was a very friendly affair: dinner, beer, guitar, and singing in four languages mixed together. And, of course, planning the climb.

This is where the first, relatively pivotal moment came. Aga wasn’t feeling well—to be precise, she felt pretty crap—so she decided to walk down with the girls. That left the five of us Mohicans. Saul immediately arranged with the lads to form a three-person team. At the time, it seemed to me a good idea. Saul is the most experienced of the three, and the team would only be stronger for it. In hindsight, I blame myself that we didn’t tell him to stay with Agnieszka; Aga was his big “project.”

After breakfast at the hut, we packed our rucksacks, took a group photo, and set off. The girls headed down; the lads headed up. None of us had any idea that this would be our last photo together, and that the girls would never see Saul, Chicho, and Arti again. Just writing this sentence brings tears to my eyes.

The second day of the climb up Huascarán was easy going. A few hundred vertical meters led up to the glacier, then a few hundred more traversed over a gently angled and relatively safe glacier to the spot where Camp 1 is set, at about 5,300m. Matěj and I arrived first, and shortly after us Arti and Chicho, with Saul behind them. We pitched the tents, melted water, cooked food, took photos, rested, and fine-tuned the details for tomorrow’s summit push.

Huascarán is normally climbed via the Garganta col, where Camp 2 is placed at 5,950m, and from there climbers make for the summit. All of us, however, were heading for the El Escudo (“The Shield”) route, which is technically harder but objectively safer,and of course more fun. It is a roughly 600-meter long ice face at 60 to 70 degrees. Thanks to its shape, there are no seracs hanging above you, no cornices, and far fewer things that can come down on your head. Unfortunately, there is little room for a Camp 2 there, so the Escudo is usually climbed from Camp 1 straight to the top, with the descent via the Normal Route.

We set our departure time for 10 pm. While the sun was still up, we crawled into our sleeping bags to get at least a little sleep and recharge the batteries a bit. We all knew that brutally hard work lay ahead.

This was it: summit day. Matěj and I got up at 9:30 p.m. so that we could leave at 10. We could hear the lads sometime before 9. In the end, we left more or less together: the lads at about 10:15 pm, we a few minutes after them.

Given the profile of the route over the Escudo, the plan from the start was to climb in pairs. Later, we became a pair and a three. You cannot climb this with five people on one rope, and none of us ever suggested it. The same applies to the descent.

The first part of the route was probably the most dangerous section of the whole ascent. It was a zone of crevasses and seracs of a size you will not encounter in Europe. There were a few sections of steep ice, but it wasn’t outright hard; it was just that something could come down at any moment. This section was shared with the Normal Route, which then branched off to the left towards Camp 2, while we headed right towards El Escudo.

We reach the bergschrund in less than two hours, with the lads following an hour or so behind us. Here we re-tied onto the full length of the 60m rope and racked up all our gear. In the process, I dropped my radio somewhere into the bowels of the glacier, and Matěj set off upward. The terrain wasn’t hard, so we simul-climbed on a single rope. Matěj climbed three to four pitches this way until he ran out of ice screws. On his last two snow pickets, he built a belay and let me through. My section was more hard snow than ice:easier climbing, less fun, but with correspondingly worse protection. I had three snow pickets on my pack, but I wanted to save those for later, for the belay anchors. After climbing some six pitches on just a handful of screws, I topped out over the lip of the Escudo, where I brought up Matýsek. (Editor’s note: “Matýsek” is a Czech nickname for “Matěj.”) By then, we were at 6,300m. It was cold as hell, with the sun still nowhere in sight. I dropped a glove and started feeling properly ill from the altitude. From this moment on, I suffered like an animal. The section from the top of the Escudo to the summit was endless; it wasn’t as steep any more, but it was long, and the soft snow did nothing to help our progress.

Sometime before 9 a.m. on Monday, July 13, though, we stood on the summit of Huascarán Sur. We didn’t really feel much joy, because we knew the worst was still ahead of us: the way down. On top of that, the summit was a completely flat plateau. You could play soccer on it, only you’d have a long way to go for a stray ball. Even so, weakness came over me and I cried. It was my fourth attempt, with my partner and childhood friend, and exactly a month to the day since our dog, Pisco, was poisoned. I cried again just remembering him. But the joy was there, too, although the main part of it was supposed to come down below. And it did come. But only for a moment.

We didn’t linger on the summit. There really was nothing there. After a snack, a little tea, and the summit photo, we headed down. No one had been on the summit for about two to three weeks, so there were no tracks and no trail. Our only guide was the offline mapy.cz app, which traced the Normal Route. But it was the route that was walked an unknown number of years ago, so we treated it as a rough indication only. We re-tied into classic glacier-travel mode, roughly 15–20 meters apart, with the rest of the rope coiled around each of our bodies.

A few minutes from the summit, on the empty, white, flat plateau, I saw something that looked like the windblown hint of a trail. Before I even got there, something happened and I was falling through the air into an icy hell.

A crevasse. With no sign whatsoever of its existence, it had been waiting there silently for its victim. I remember precisely the feeling of flying into the void. It was as if I were falling for an eternity. Suddenly, I felt the rope come tight, and I knew that was Matýsek. But I keep falling. In that instant, I was certain that in a moment, Matýsek would come flying in after me and we’d both be dead.

But a miracle happened—a miracle called Matěj. The rope bit again and I stopped. He bloody well caught me. I was hanging about 12–15 meters down in a crevasse that runs 100m in each direction and could easily be 50m, 100m, or 300m deep. Who knows? Below me, I saw nothing but icy hell. The crevasse was completely covered with a cap of fine snow. Zero chance of seeing it.

To my own surprise, I didn’t panic; I knew exactly what to do. My only connection to our world was the rope I was hanging on and whatever Matěj had rigged up there to keep me in this world. I clipped a Micro Traxion onto my harness as a self-belay and climbed up the vertical ice wall towards the surface as far as I could. I felt so OK that I even took three photos inside the crevasse. When the line of the rope stopped me from climbing any farther, I swung out into space. I ascended the rest on prusiks.

In a little under half an hour, I was out, back on the surface, back in the world. Only now did I breathe out.

Matěj and I fell into each other’s arms and began to grasp what we just survived. It would have taken very little for him to come in after me, and no one would ever have found us. In hindsight, I see it as an enormous miracle and a measure of enormous skill on Matěj’s part. He saved our lives. And maybe it was at this very moment that we realized this really was no joke and that things were getting serious. We continued the descent very cautiously, and we were genuinely scared.

We reached the edge of an ice wall where the Normal Route was supposed to be, but there was nothing there—nothing at all. There was no question of searching for a way through this labyrinth. The wall looked to be about 20m high, with a ramp below it along which we hoped to traverse off left toward the Normal Route.

We rappelled off two snow pickets into a relatively safe spot surrounded by seracs. I remember looking at the leaning towers next to us and thinking that one deep breath would be enough to bring the whole lot down. Still, we felt a certain safety in our little hollow. Finally, we had a bit of time to catch our breath, eat and drink a little, and get in touch with the lads.

Before I could do that, they called me. They said they were about 200 vertical meters below the summit. But it was already late, around 12 to 1 p.m., so their plan was to traverse off towards the Normal Route and descend by it.

I told them to try to find our tracks and our rappel, and to really watch out for crevasses, because I had just fallen into one. We confirmed that everything was OK and that we would meet in the evening at Camp 1, or if need be, at base camp, depending on the time and our energy. If I had known that this was the last time I would speak to them, I would have told them so much more.

A little later, while searching for the route on the map, my phone slipped out of my hand and vanished somewhere below. But I didn’t dwell on it. I knew we had other worries. It was just a shame to lose the photos from inside the crevasse.

Another unpleasant rappel off a 25-meter serac, and we were on the slope leading towards the Garganta. By now the weather had turned and visibility had dropped to a few tens of meters. But we were already in the Garganta, at 6,000m. All we needed now was to find Camp 2, which was just a flat patch where tents stood two days ago. From there, there should be some tracks and a route down.

We wandered about a bit, but eventually we found Camp 2 and there really was a hint of tracks. Shortly before that, the summit shook off its cloud, and we saw the lads above us at about 6,300 or 6,400 m. It even looked to us as though they were closer to the Normal Route than we were. We were really relieved.

The route from Camp 2 to Camp 1 is the most dangerous crux of the climb. It is a zone of avalanches, collapsing seracs, and crevasses. Later, Matěj and I agreed that we both felt as if we were living in some kind of virtual reality, outside our bodies, just waiting for the video game to end. Maybe that was why I didn’t really register the risk.

We arrived back at Camp 1 at sunset. With our last reserves of strength, we packed up and continued down to the refugio. A hot dinner and a bed was, after all, a big draw. And it was only a little under two hours more.

We reached the hut sometime before nine in the evening, after 23 hours on our feet. We asked for something to eat and a beer, then went to sleep. We thought about the lads and somehow had no doubt that they were fine and that they were at Camp 1. After all, we saw them a short way above Camp 2.

In the morning, we woke up at the hut completely wrecked, but suddenly also happy with what we’d achieved. We expected the lads to turn up any moment, and then we’d race down into the valley. The news from the lads, however, was different.

Saul wrote that they couldn’t find the way down and had to bivouac somewhere above Camp 2. But they were OK, he said; they survived the night up there without any camping gear. They were tired, Chicho was apparently feeling ill, but all were OK. They would try to find the descent route. From then on, we were to communicate with Chicho, as Saul’s battery was dead.

We still had no idea that things were bad. But neither did the lads. Because things weren’t bad yet. We managed to download our GPS track onto a phone and send it to the lads as a mapy.cz link. Up above Camp 2, there was occasionally a signal, so we communicated with the lads via WhatsApp.

At 9:10 a.m., Chicho wrote asking us to send our route. At 9:15 a.m., we sent the mapy.cz link and it was delivered.

At 10:16 am, Chicho sent a photo and a video from where they were. They sounded fine; Camp 2 was visible below them. At 10:18 a.m., we sent them a crib sheet of roughly which way to go down, including a sketch drawn onto their photo. This was delivered. At 10:21 a.m., we asked Chicho whether he had our route on mapy.cz. This was the last message delivered to Chicho. After 10:21 am, no further messages were delivered. From then on, there was no communication from them.

Since the weather was beautiful, the lads were fine, and there was no signal lower down towards Camp 2, it never occurred to us that the lads might be in trouble. We were certain they had descended lower, lost signal, had flat phones, and so on—but were descending. There was essentially no reason to think anything else. They werebujacos”—tough guys. They had survived a night without sleeping bags at 6,400m, and now they would just run down to us, to the girls, to lunch, to a beer.

Given these assumptions, we knew we had several hours of waiting before they would appear above Camp 1, which could be seen from the hut. Meanwhile, we tried to contact them by radio and by phone, in case they happened to come into signal. But still, we were completely calm.

When about 3 p.m. came and there was no sign of them, though, it seemed odd to us. It had gone on too long. We decided we would go up and look for them. They were probably tired and frozen, and another night up there would be truly critical.

We ate, brewed tea, and took all the clothing we had. In the meantime, I called a friend, a guide, down in town so that he knew something was going on and could start rounding up help if needed. But there was still a chance alive in us that they were at Camp 1, asleep in the tent, and simply had no way of letting us know. Down in town, a group of volunteers was already getting ready to set out to help. There is no organized professional mountain rescue in Peru.

But there was no one at Camp 1. By then we sensed that things were bad; we just didn’t know how bad. On top of that, a snowstorm was moving in.

The message we sent down was clear: It was official, we needed help. The lads were somewhere up there. We were going up for them. We knew that down in the valley things were already under way and would keep going without us. But we had a different task: to find them and bring them down.

We pack all their sleeping bags as well and set off up into the gale. Matěj created a Garmin messaging group and made our position trackable online. He realized more than I did that we were heading into real trouble.

I have friends up there – my “churaks,” my “hermanos.” I registered nothing.

For the first time in my life, I was faster than Matěj. In my head, I knew they were up there, frozen and exhausted but alive, and that we were their only chance.

In these conditions I would turn around on any climb of my own; I would never go on. But now it wasn’t about me. It was about the lads. The moment we gave up, they would have no chance.

We got as far as above Camp 2, to 6,000m, but there was nothing anywhere. That was shortly before midnight. Just gale, snowfall, a temperature I’d estimate at around minus 30 degrees, and not a trace of the lads.

By this point I knew things were very bad. In this weather, no one survives up here for even an hour. Matýsek was very afraid of going down because of the avalanche danger and pushed for us to stay at Camp 2 and somehow sit it out with all those sleeping bags. I knew that if we stayed, we would be dead within the hour. In the end, I convinced him that we had to go down. That was about 50/50. But staying up there felt to me like 90/10.

The descent was horrific. Avalanche terrain was everywhere we looked, with giant seracs over our heads almost the entire way. Visibility was near zero, and our tracks drifted over with snow. Nevertheless, our guardian angel was on duty again and guided us down. We get back to Camp 1 sometime around 3 a.m. on Wednesday, July 15. There I well and truly collapsed, mentally and physically. We crawled into the tent for a while and waited for the first group of volunteer rescuers, who, as we already knew, were on their way from the refugio.

Even in the tent and in a sleeping bag, 700 vertical meters lower, the weather was terrible, and it was here that Matýsek told me we were one–all. He had saved us above that crevasse, and I had by insisting that we go down.

The first rescue team reached Camp 1 just after first light, sometime after 6 a.m. It was five people in all, three guides and two aspirant guides, and among them my good friend Szymon, who was the only one closer to me than Saul. That was all. We handed over all the information we had and let them go up. We would head for the refugio, where further teams were supposedly bound: one to set up a support base at Camp 2, another at Camp 1. Supposedly lots of people were coming.

Unfortunately, we were in Peru, where everything does not quite work. We met the Camp 1 team below the glacier—five lads, all friends, climbers, and volunteers from Peru, Colombia, Spain, and the Czech Republic. No Camp 2 team ever came, and in fact, there never was one. The five lads up above had packed late in the evening and set out from Huaraz to help friends in need. In a single push they climbed to over 6,000m. But because of the lack of support and the conditions up there, they had to turn back in the afternoon and descend to Camp 1. That essentially ended any realistic chance of finding the lads alive. I personally had known already at Camp 2, which we had climbed to in our attempt to find them, that there was almost no chance left.

From then on, great chaos set in across the whole organization of the rescue and search. As I already mentioned, Peru has no mountain rescue service; there are no rescue helicopters, no rescuers, no system, no protocol, no designated person to take charge of any coordination. Everything happened through the WhatsApp group that Matěj created when we set out for the lads. Everything was done on the basis of volunteers, friends, guides and family members.

Eventually, the police, the high-mountain police (Policía de Alta Montaña), and apparently even the army joined in to help. But their help consisted solely of transporting volunteers by helicopter from the town of Caraz to the refugio at 4,650m, and the first helicopter took off 24 hours after the official call for help. What’s more, it flew up empty, to assess the situation, only to find that another police helicopter had been sitting on the “helipad” by the refugio for several days, badly parked, and that its pilots were afraid to take off in it.

In the end, some transport up did take place, but effectively only on the following days. If someone down below had been able to coordinate it, they could have sent the line of volunteers towards base camp on foot, with a herd of donkeys, on the very first day of the search (July 15), and they would have been there far sooner than waiting until the next day to see whether anyone would get a place on the helicopter.

The entire rescue, and subsequently the search, was run by volunteers and the families. It was they who hired private companies with advanced drones, they who coordinated everything, mainly with the police, they who did everything they could. I myself went back with two volunteers a few days later to a point just above Camp 1 to attempt a further search with drones—without success. The zone where all the clues ended—GPS signals, the location of the last photos, the hint of the last tracks, and so on—is at around 6,500m. Camp 1 is at 5,300m, and most drones simply cannot fly that high and that far. Only the advanced police drones can, and they in turn lacked experienced pilots willing to risk it. Going higher wasn’t possible because of the conditions on the mountain. So, a stalemate. In the end footage from the suspect zone was obtained, but there was not a trace of the lads.

After almost detective-like work by many friends—using the GPS signal from their watch as requested from Garmin headquarters, footage from private and police drones, plus footage from the guides who went all the way up during the search, information from us, and the lads’ last photos—it appears that the lads missed our belay station by literally a few meters, and that while crossing a snow bridge over gigantic crevasse systems it collapsed beneath them.

When I first wrote this report, it had already been more than three weeks since I last saw the lads, since I last spoke to them. Still nothing has been found today, and in my view it will be hard for anything to be found. The scale of everything on Huascarán is utterly absurd: crevasses several hundred meters wide and deep, seracs the size of apartment blocks… Sure, this isn’t the Himalaya, and it doesn’t look like it, either. Huascarán looks like two lazy fat blokes lying side by side. But beware of it. That mountain is mean and hostile, and although on paper it isn’t hard—the Normal Route was reportedly graded only AD/AD+, the Escudo D—it is no game. You are playing for your life at every single step.

I don’t think that either we or the lads made any fundamental mistake; it was simply that Matýsek and I had a large dose of luck, while the lads had a little less of it.

I am not writing this story for sensation. It is just one real story with a tragic ending. If at least a few of you take something from it—ideally some kind of lesson—it will have served its purpose. As we all know, the mountains give, but sometimes they also take away. We unfortunately now know that this is no empty phrase but the hard truth.

Editor’s note: This trip report has been translated from the original Czech into English with permission of the author. Place names and local terms (La Garganta, El Escudo, refugio, Huaracino, pollo a la brasa) have not been translated from the original Spanish.

Bryce Harper’s controversial comments receive praise from ex-players

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Bryce Harper's controversial comments receive praise from ex-players appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Recently, Philadelphia Phillies slugger Bryce Harper made startling comments about the 2027 MLB season. Essentially, he believes there won't be a season because MLB may lock out during collective bargaining negotiations.

The sentiment caught many by surprise, but it resonated with Anthony Rizzo and David Ross, per Ross and Rizzo Unfiltered. They both said they understood where he was coming from, but not in the way most would think.

While talking to fans on social media, Harper said, "It will be this year," in response to a fan who said the offseason runs from March to October. Another fan chimed in to say that Harper "likes the offseason more than the season atp"

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

That met Rizzo and Ross where they were, since they expressed similar enjoyment of the offseason.

"The offseason is nice after a 162-game grind," Ross said.

"The offseason is way better than the season, are you kidding me?" Rizzo said.

"You get that downtime that is so important to the mental and physical health of the players," Ross said.

Ultimately, Rizzo agreed wholeheartedly with Harper, but added that the regular season is enjoyable for the love of the game.

"The season is now is the greatest thing ever," Rizzo said. "I love playing baseball. Love it. There's nothing I would have rather done during the year."

Rizzo and Ross were two of the most popular players from the 2016 Chicago Cubs that won the World Series.

The 2027 season hinges on whether the MLB Players Association (MLBPA) and the owners can agree on the salary cap. The owners are pushing for a salary floor, while the MLBPA opposes a cap.

If they don't reach a deal by Spring Training, the season could be in jeopardy.

Patrick Dumont family became controlling owners of Mavericks last month

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Sivan and Patrick Dumont purchased Miriam Adelson's shares of the Dallas Mavericks last month, making the Dumont family the controlling owners of the franchise with a 69% share. Adelson has had minimal involvement with the Mavericks since she bought the majority share of the franchise from Mark Cuban in December 2023 along with her daughter and son-in-law. Patrick Dumont has served as the Mavericks' governor since the families took control of the franchise. "It's really about long-term investment in the city of Dallas," Dumont told ESPN on Tuesday. "The Dumont family is really committed to building what we hope is the best arena in the NBA, a really significant investment in the city that will help create the new 40-year home for the Dallas Mavericks, a great experience for our fans and inspire our team to win championships. That's really what we're focused on. We had the opportunity, and we're really excited about it."
